<h3>3. Backlog tools</h3>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>Q: Who here has discovered a way of organizing their backlog that they’re pretty happy with? We're migrating ours to Asana, and starting fresh! Curious what kinds of mental models or frameworks folks here have found success with</strong></p>
<p><strong>– <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/kali-borkoski-998082171/">Kali Borkoski</a></strong></p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong><a href="https://twitter.com/wplusv">Wayne Vernon</a></strong>: At a super high level, my backlog goals are:</p>
<ol>
<li>Development work only that is in a workable stage, i.e. can be picked up a ran with by a dev based on the information in the ticket</li>
<li>If it’s dev work but <em>not</em> ready to be worked on, consider setting up an Icebox so your team can ignore it but you don’t lose it</li>
<li>If it’s not dev work but an idea, feature request, etc. instead, it should live somewhere other than your backlog. Plus, schedule intentional time to groom your backlog.</li>
</ol>
<p>I used to try to do this as part of other ceremonies but it never really worked out. Now I have 30 minutes every 2 weeks where myself, my leads, and QA sort the backlog by last activity and work through closing, updating, etc. as many tickets as possible. It’s infrequent and short enough to not be painful but enough to slowly chip away at all the noise.</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/kali-borkoski-998082171/">Kali Borkoski</a></strong>: Thank you, @Wayne Vernon! What do you do with the tickets that fall in bucket #3?</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://twitter.com/wplusv">Wayne Vernon</a></strong>: I personally maintain my own list in a doc (Confluence for us) that I prioritize and add context to. There are definitely better ways to do this, e.g. most product management tools handle feature ideas and requests more natively. Really depends on your “customer” and how visible you need to make these things. For me, I’m not super worried about it so a doc works fine.</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/yairlivne/">Yair</a></strong>: At Quora we use Asana for tracking backlogs. Each team has its own backlog &quot;project&quot; where tasks are tracked. Teams usually have some cadence where the PMs/eng managers might go through the backlog tasks, reprioritize / potentially delete some / potentially just crank a few out. This is particularly useful ahead of weeks where we focus on product/tech debt and there's time to plow through a bunch of these.</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/kali-borkoski-998082171/">Kali Borkoski</a></strong>: @Yair, Thank you! What are the fields you've added to that Asana board? (Priority, effort, etc?)</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/yairlivne/">Yair</a></strong>: This was team-by-team, but I do think almost everyone has priorities, as well as topical tags.</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://twitter.com/karrisaarinen">Karri Saarinen</a></strong>: We are makers of <a href="https://linear.app/">Linear</a>. Our framework is that you add all the new work to the backlog which means that’s only an idea but not committed work yet. Then you can have priorities or a few different categories like icebox, backlog, next that categorize generally how relevant this work is. Then for each cycle (sprint) we just select the work from the backlog to be worked on next.</p>
<p>Overall we don’t worry or groom the backlog too much, we just add stuff and then in the cycle planning select what is relevant to work on next. So backlog is the dump of all the possible work we could do, and cycles define what we actually do. With that thinking you don’t need spent time organizing everything perfectly in the backlog, especially because most of it doesn’t ever get done.</p>
<p>We also have a feature that autocloses issues after x months so the backlogs don’t get infinite. If an issue or bug doesn’t get done in 6mo, it likely never gets done and if it’s important, it will surface again.</p>
<p><strong>Gaurav Chandrashekar</strong>: @Wayne, don't you have backlog grooming as part of the sprint ceremonies?</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://twitter.com/wplusv">Wayne Vernon</a></strong>: @Gaurav, we follow a fairly casual implementation of agile at our org that is closer to Kanban than anything. Some like to have the “backlog groom” and pre-planning for the next sprint(s) be one and the same. Some alternate these week to week. I’ve personally had better luck setting aside completely separate time where we do nothing but try to revise/expand/close/prioritize old tickets in the backlog.TLDR: I’m not an agile expert or advocate. I generally take a high-context approach where I do what I feel will get the best results from the team and product I’m working with at the time. And I’m fortunate to work in an org that supports this approach.</p>
<h3>4. Tools for scheduling user interviews</h3>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>Q: Who has a tool they like for scheduling/coordinating user interviews? We're looking at UserTesting but would love alternatives.</strong></p>
<p><strong>– <a href="https://twitter.com/herzigma">Josh</a></strong></p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong><a href="https://twitter.com/ttrauser">Manuel Bruschi</a></strong>: We use Calendly as a team with a round-robin assignment.</p>
<p><strong>Tanmayi Sai</strong>: I use this <strong><a href="https://lookback.io/">Lookback</a></strong> after switching over from User Interviews. It's also good for unmoderated tests**.**</p>
<p><strong>Scott Stenback</strong>: Hi Josh - I know a founder who is building a new product around improving the scheduling experience. Let me know if you'd like a connection: <a href="https://www.buildbetter.app/">https://www.buildbetter.app/</a></p>
<h3>5. Building an <strong>“idea-repository”</strong></h3>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>Q: Does anyone have a cool template for an “idea-repository”? I’m rotating to a new team and after talking with the UX teams I’ll be working with, they mentioned sometimes the ideas they propose in the projects get lost. I’m looking for some template or dynamic to tease this out. Something simple and clean that would allow everyone to express their view. I’m extremely interested in the ideas that come from UX teams.</strong></p>
<p><strong>–</strong><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/frandangelo/">Franco</a></p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong><a href="https://twitter.com/herzigma">Josh</a></strong>: What tools do you use to capture ideas from other stakeholders, like user requests or executive requests? If you have a tool like that, then that's one good place.</p>
<p>If your only tool (i.e., a tool that gets used and worked actively and on a daily basis) is your backlog tool (like Jira) then I'd schedule a couple of hours for brainstorming and prioritizing meeting. The diverge/converge framework from design sprints is pretty good for this. And then just prioritize their feedback for the backlog.</p>
<p>Is this with the team you're rotating off or rotating on to? Either way might be nice to have the other PM (who you're replacing or who is replacing you) participate.</p>
<p><strong>Sebastian Leon</strong>: Agreed with Josh, integrating idea-collection into an existing workflow is key!</p>
<p>Ultimately, you need a list that anyone can add to, with 2 items per idea: 1) quick description, 2) link to doc with more context (optional). This can be done in Jira, Google Spreadsheets, Notion, etc. Anytime someone brings up an idea, ask them to add it to the list.</p>
<p>For item 2), ask your team to fill out one of these templates (<a href="https://www.lennyrachitsky.com/p/my-favorite-templates-issue-37">link</a>). It’s actually pretty nice when backlog items are developed and matured over time, so that once it makes sense to prioritize it, a lot of the product thinking &amp; interesting decisions have already been done**.**</p>

<h1><strong>🎤 AMA with</strong> Pete Kazanjy (about sales)</h1>
<h3>1. <strong>Learning how to do sales</strong></h3>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>Q: Maybe not such a good question... Have you read Demand Side Sales? If so curious what you think. If not, it’s basically Jobs To Be Done product theory applied to sales. I’d like to understand how people (you) educate themselves around sales, because like product management, you can’t really “go to school for it”</strong></p>
<p><strong>– <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/david-m-krell-508a3331/">David Krell</a></strong></p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/kazanjy/">Pete Kazanjy</a></strong>: I haven't read Demand Side sales, but sounds great! You're right that there isn't a &quot;sales degree&quot; per se, and that's in part what makes it challenging to learn later in your career, as most of the knowledge is learned via apprenticeship.</p>
<p>I learned a lot via First Round's Network, which was an early peer education community, which inspired me to create Modern Sales (<a href="https://modernsaleshq.com/">https://modernsaleshq.com/</a>) which is the sales operations and leadership community my company, Atrium, runs. 20k people. But peer education is key.</p>
<p>And then lastly ongoing learning of course is important. This is why I wrote Founding Sales - to reduce the learning curve: <a href="https://www.foundingsales.com/">https://www.foundingsales.com/</a>. Other great books are:</p>
<ul>
<li>Cracking the Sales Management Code</li>
<li>The Transparency Sale</li>
<li>The Challenger Sale</li>
<li>To Sell Is Human</li>
<li>Persuasion by Robert Cialdini</li>
</ul>
<h3>2. Managing lead flow</h3>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>Q: What systems \ process have you seen best for managing lead flow? Lot’s of clients signup, some should have sale engage. What is the best systems for pushing the right leads over to Sales?</strong></p>
<p><strong>– <a href="https://twitter.com/meetalijavid">Ali Javid</a></strong></p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/kazanjy/">Pete Kazanjy</a></strong>: Depends on the volume, but check out this chapter on &quot;Inbound Marketing&quot; from <strong><a href="https://www.foundingsales.com/6-inbound-marketing">Chapter 6 - Early Inbound Marketing: Inbound Lead Capture &amp; Response - Startup Inbound Basics — Founding Sales</a></strong></p>
<p>The first step is to have a mechanism by which to capture signups and hand raising. Having a &quot;Contact Sales&quot; button on the home page, even if it's a bootie mailto: link is important, but the more sophisticated version is to have a lead form that flows into your CRM. For Self-Serve, we talked about this a LOT in the post I wrote for Lenny's newsletter, but getting signup data into a CRM in some capacity, usually via a syncing tool like Census (Segment =&gt; Redshift / Snowflake =&gt; Hubspot CRM / Salesforce etc.) is powerful. You want to get the leads into a place where a human can prioritize them/triage/act.</p>
<h3>3. Sales as an introvert</h3>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>Q: I’ve always felt like folks who are good at sales are extroverted… do you know of more introverted people doing well in sales also? If so, what helps them succeed and there are any “classic introvert” traits that are actually an advantage?</strong></p>
<p><strong>– <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/karaskrip/">Kara Skrip</a></strong></p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/kazanjy/">Pete Kazanjy</a></strong>: Definitely. I talk about this some in this deck, starting at this slide:  <a href="https://docs.google.com/presentation/d/1pcSy-zV-776abGmZ8WJ7bGeXcHQAxscdypGdrUz28_c/edit#slide=id.g168e5f5d53_0_14">Founder Led Selling Maturity Stages - Founding Sales.</a> In short, yeah, you can see why someone who's extroverted would take more naturally to a task that requires to talk to lots of people they don't know.</p>
<p>That said, the way I like to describe &quot;modern salespeople&quot; is as &quot;consultants who happen to have a predisposition to one particular solution (theirs)&quot;.</p>
<p>So analysis, writing, math, logic, argumentation, etc. all really becomes key there. And being good at those things is way more important than just being &quot;fun.&quot; But getting good at / comfortable with selling behaviors does indeed require work, and you just build those muscles over time through lots of repetition.</p>
<p>More here: <a href="https://www.foundingsales.com/1-mindset-changes">Chapter 1 - Mindset Changes: Startup Sales Mindset Changes — Founding Sales</a></p>
<p><strong><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/karaskrip/">Kara Skrip</a></strong>: Yeah I can see the &quot;doing your homework&quot; or prep portion of the job being really good for introverts</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/kazanjy/">Pete Kazanjy</a></strong>: &quot;Doing your homework&quot; (pre-call planning, proposal creation, etc.) is where a huge amount of the work is done.</p>
<h3>4. Self-serve product experience</h3>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>Q: Hi Pete - in your post you talked about “is the product simple enough for self-serve?” - how do you differentiate between “not an easy product to use” versus a product that is just poorly designed? My gut is complexity of product does not necessarily = poor user experience. Can you talk a bit about this? Thank you in advance!</strong></p>
<p><strong>– Anonymous</strong></p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/kazanjy/">Pete Kazanjy</a></strong>: This is really more of a product question, really, but I think that there's a linear relationship between the complexity of a problem being solved and the complexity of an associated solution. With great product and engineering effort you can of course abstract that away with elegance and simplicity, but the more complicated the problem, probably the more engineering and product effort to do so. So I think the point here is that you should be mindful of this, and be honest when looking at your activation metrics to see if you are indeed getting there with self-serve - and if not...maybe it needs more human earlier.</p>
<h3>5. Frameworks for doing large enterprise sales</h3>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>Q: Hi Pete - are there any frameworks / processes that you recommend for doing large enterprise sales i.e. complex, lots of stakeholders, very intensive but large ACV? For example, does value based selling really work where you try and break down the solution into granular cost saving or revenue generating items to discuss with prospects.</strong></p>
<p><strong>– <a href="https://twitter.com/nazking15">Nawaz Imam</a></strong></p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/kazanjy/">Pete Kazanjy</a></strong>: @Nawaz, are you talking about top-down enterprise selling?</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://twitter.com/nazking15">Nawaz Imam</a></strong>: I'm guessing it could either be top down or bottom up depending on the type of organisation? Seems most likely to be top down though.</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/kazanjy/">Pete Kazanjy</a></strong>: For those larger orgs, being crisper about deal modeling, using a methodology like MEDDIC, for example, can be helpful: <a href="https://www.lucidchart.com/blog/close-more-deals-with-meddic-sales-process#:~:text=MEDDIC%20is%20an%20acronym%20that,customer%20into%20your%20sales%20funnel">Close more deals with the MEDDIC sales process</a>.</p>

<h2>[56/61] Generating buzz</h2>
<p><em>Below is a peek at this week’s 🔒 <strong>subscriber-only</strong> 🔒 post. <a href="https://www.lennyrachitsky.com/subscribe?coupon=dac5b98c">Subscribe</a> to read the full post.</em></p>
<blockquote>
<h2><strong>Q: I’m planning to launch my product soon, and wondering how companies generate buzz when they launch?</strong></h2>
</blockquote>
<p>Here’s the hard truth: No one cares about your new product.</p>
<p>As Marc Andreessen said, “everyone’s time is already allocated.” With <a href="https://www.producthunt.com/">hundreds of products</a> launching each day and endless ads clamoring for our attention, how do you get over the event horizon of attention? Easy: <strong>DO SOMETHING REMARKABLE.</strong></p>
<blockquote>
<p>“Remarkable is a really cool word, because we think it just means neat, but it also means <strong>worth making a remark about.</strong>”</p>
<p>ー Seth Godin</p>
</blockquote>
<p>Your goal is to create <em>something worth remarking about</em>. Something people will stop and pay attention to, and <em>have</em> to share with their friends. Seth Godin explores this idea further (starting at 6:58):</p>
<p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xBIVlM435Zg">Watch on YouTube</a></p>
<p>Great, be remarkable. Thanks, Lenny! How the hell do you do that? Well, I’ve got some answers. I spent the past few weeks collecting and categorizing the buzziest launches I could find. <strong>Below, you’ll find the ten most common strategies for launching</strong><em><strong>something worth remarking about</strong></em> (including 49 real-life examples).</p>
<p><img src="https://bucketeer-e05bbc84-baa3-437e-9518-adb32be77984.s3.amazonaws.com/public/images/ff8bd15f-c93c-409b-9119-ab0e5c6c13f8_2400x1350.png" alt="Image from Generating buzz"></p>
<p>I hope the examples below inspire you and your team to create something remarkable ✨</p>
<p>Let’s dive in.</p>

<li><strong><a href="https://www.inc.com/magazine/201505/graham-winfrey/neil-blumenthal-icons-of-entrepreneurship.html">Warby Parker</a></strong>: “Try on five pairs of frames at home for free before buying any”</li>
<li><strong><a href="https://web.archive.org/web/20110614213044/https://www.spotify.com/int/">Spotify</a></strong>: “All of the world’s music – for free”</li>

<li><strong><a href="https://web.archive.org/web/20150228021831/https://www.eero.com/">Eero</a></strong>: &quot;Finally, WiFi that works”</li>

</ol>

</ol>
<p><img src="https://bucketeer-e05bbc84-baa3-437e-9518-adb32be77984.s3.amazonaws.com/public/images/99772841-abd9-4ab0-9319-0133a9218755_1273x714.png" alt="Image from Generating buzz"></p>

<li><strong><a href="https://www.theverge.com/2020/3/4/21165312/quibi-funding-investors-jeffrey-katzenberg-launch-date-price">Quibi</a></strong>: Celebrity founders raise ~$2B for a pre-product video platform</li>
<li><strong><a href="https://twitter.com/dhh/status/1272968382329942017?lang=en">Hey.com</a></strong>: Public figure calling out Apple for their App Store fees</li>

<li><strong><a href="https://money.cnn.com/2006/02/08/real_estate/money_zillow/">Zillow</a></strong>: Zestimates telling you the value of your home</li>

<h2>[57/61] Generating buzz</h2>
<blockquote>
<h2>Q: I’m planning to launch my product soon, and wondering how companies generate buzz when they launch?</h2>
</blockquote>
<p>Here’s the hard truth: No one cares about your new product.</p>
<p>As Marc Andreessen said, “everyone’s time is already allocated.” With <a href="https://www.producthunt.com/">hundreds of products</a> launching each day and endless ads clamoring for our attention, how do you get over the event horizon of attention? Easy: <strong>DO SOMETHING REMARKABLE.</strong></p>
<blockquote>
<p>“Remarkable is a really cool word, because we think it just means neat, but it also means <strong>worth making a remark about.</strong>”</p>
<p>ー Seth Godin</p>
</blockquote>
<p>Your goal is to create <em>something</em> <em>worth remarking about</em>. Something people will stop and pay attention to, and <em>have</em> to share with their friends. Seth Godin explores this idea further (starting at 6:58m):</p>
<p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=xBIVlM435Zg">Watch on YouTube</a></p>
<p>Great, be remarkable. Thanks, Lenny! How the hell do you do that? Well, I’ve got some answers. I spent the past few weeks collecting and categorizing the buzziest launches I could find. <strong>Below, you’ll find the ten most common strategies for launching</strong> <em><strong>something worth remarking about</strong></em> (including 49 real-life examples).</p>
<p><img src="https://bucketeer-e05bbc84-baa3-437e-9518-adb32be77984.s3.amazonaws.com/public/images/ff8bd15f-c93c-409b-9119-ab0e5c6c13f8_2400x1350.png" alt="Image from Generating buzz"></p>
<p>I hope the examples below inspire you and your team to create something remarkable ✨</p>
<p>Let’s dive in.</p>
<h2><strong>1. Release a remarkable video</strong></h2>
<ol>
<li><strong><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ZKLnhuzh9uY">Poo Pourii</a></strong></li>
</ol>
<p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ZKLnhuzh9uY">Watch on YouTube</a></p>

<li><strong><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Bl9wqNe5J8U">Descript</a></strong></li>
</ol>
<p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Bl9wqNe5J8U">Watch on YouTube</a></p>
<ol start="3">
<li><strong><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ZUG9qYTJMsI">Dollar Shave Club</a></strong></li>
</ol>
<p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ZUG9qYTJMsI">Watch on YouTube</a></p>
<h2><strong>2. Create a remarkable product demo</strong></h2>
<ol start="4">
<li><strong><a href="https://t.co/V3YVgj3kTB?amp=1">mmhmm</a></strong></li>
</ol>
<p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=c8KhKBLoSMk">Watch on YouTube</a></p>
<ol start="5">

</ol>
<p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7QmCUDHpNzE">Watch on YouTube</a></p>

<li><strong><a href="https://www.theverge.com/2012/12/13/3759494/mailbox-for-iphone-email-app">Mailbox</a></strong></li>
</ol>
<p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=_Us1Ws4i14c">Watch on YouTube</a></p>
<h2><strong>3. Offer a remarkable value prop</strong></h2>
<ol start="7">
<li><strong><a href="https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=6906146">Robinhood</a></strong>: “$0 commission stock brokerage”</li>
<li><strong><a href="https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=8863">Dropbox</a></strong>: “Throw away your USB drive”</li>
<li><strong><a href="https://googlepress.blogspot.com/2004/04/google-gets-message-launches-gmail.html">Gmail</a></strong>: “Free email with 1GB of storage”</li>
<li><strong><a href="https://medium.com/better-marketing/how-netflix-destroyed-blockbuster-in-just-6-years-4c5c3006fe3e">Netflix</a></strong>: “No Due Dates, No Late Fees”</li>
<li><strong><a href="https://www.inc.com/magazine/201505/graham-winfrey/neil-blumenthal-icons-of-entrepreneurship.html">Warby Parker</a></strong>: “Try on five pairs of frames at home for free before buying any”</li>
<li><strong><a href="https://web.archive.org/web/20110614213044/https://www.spotify.com/int/">Spotify</a></strong>: “All of the world’s music – for free”</li>
<li><strong><a href="https://gigaom.com/2013/11/14/meet-coin-a-startup-creating-a-universal-credit-card/">Coin</a></strong>: “Replace eight of your credit cards with just one”</li>
<li><strong><a href="https://web.archive.org/web/20150228021831/https://www.eero.com/">Eero</a></strong>: &quot;Finally, WiFi that works”</li>
</ol>
<h2><strong>4. Execute a remarkable offline stunt</strong></h2>
<ol start="15">
<li><strong><a href="https://www.wired.com/1999/12/welcome-to-half-com-oregon/">Half.com</a></strong> renamed a town in Oregon to “Half.com” for a year</li>
</ol>
<p><img src="https://bucketeer-e05bbc84-baa3-437e-9518-adb32be77984.s3.amazonaws.com/public/images/7ef6fe5d-b50a-47a8-90bc-d789b1db973f_500x374.jpeg" alt="What Ever Happened to Half.com, Oregon?: Design Observer"></p>
<ol start="16">
<li><strong><a href="https://www.businessinsider.com/marc-benioff-salesforcecom-chief-has-pulled-some-crazy-stunts-2012-3">Salesforce</a></strong> hired fake protesters, commandeered all the taxis at a rival’s event and used them to deliver a 45-minute pitch, etc.</li>
</ol>
<p><img src="https://bucketeer-e05bbc84-baa3-437e-9518-adb32be77984.s3.amazonaws.com/public/images/df69759f-9f6c-44dc-8856-75cda0634596_400x298.jpeg" alt="Salesforce logo fake picket"></p>
<ol start="17">
<li><strong><a href="https://techcrunch.com/2010/10/26/wepay-ice-paypal/amp/">WePay</a></strong> dropped 600 pounds of ice in front of PayPal’s conference</li>
</ol>
<p><img src="https://bucketeer-e05bbc84-baa3-437e-9518-adb32be77984.s3.amazonaws.com/public/images/739f2353-ac89-429a-9617-d3f3ee15929e_629x841.png" alt="Image from Generating buzz"></p>
<ol start="18">
<li><strong><a href="https://www.theverge.com/2016/11/10/13584488/snapchat-snap-inc-spectacles-vending-machine-snapbot-available">Snapchat</a></strong> dropped off strange vending machines at random locations in LA with a limited number of Spectacles</li>
</ol>
<p><img src="https://bucketeer-e05bbc84-baa3-437e-9518-adb32be77984.s3.amazonaws.com/public/images/e74ad690-0795-499c-9896-7bd869d64218_700x381.png" alt="Snapbot"></p>
<ol start="19">
<li><strong><a href="https://www.adweek.com/creativity/baby-brand-tommee-tippee-made-baby-wipes-reams-actual-parenting-advice-166428/">Tommee Tippee</a></strong> turned parenting help books into limited-edition baby wipes.</li>
</ol>
<p><img src="https://bucketeer-e05bbc84-baa3-437e-9518-adb32be77984.s3.amazonaws.com/public/images/99772841-abd9-4ab0-9319-0133a9218755_1273x714.png" alt="Image from Generating buzz"></p>
<h2><strong>5. Start a remarkable controversy</strong></h2>
<ol start="20">
<li><strong><a href="https://www.businessinsider.com/everyone-is-going-crazy-over-yo-app-2014-6">Yo</a></strong>: An app that only lets you send “Yo” to your friends raises $1m</li>
<li><strong><a href="https://www.theverge.com/2020/3/4/21165312/quibi-funding-investors-jeffrey-katzenberg-launch-date-price">Quibi</a></strong>: Celebrity founders raise ~$2B for a pre-product video platform</li>
<li><strong><a href="https://twitter.com/dhh/status/1272968382329942017?lang=en">Hey.com</a></strong>: Public figure calling out Apple for their App Store fees</li>
<li><strong><a href="https://techcrunch.com/2016/01/14/buzzy-social-app-stolen-shuts-down-amidst-concerns-from-users/">Stolen</a></strong>: An app that lets you “buy” your friends on Twitter</li>
<li><strong><a href="https://money.cnn.com/2006/02/08/real_estate/money_zillow/">Zillow</a></strong>: Zestimates telling you the value of your home</li>
</ol>
<h2><strong>6. Offer a remarkable giveaway</strong></h2>
<ol start="25">
<li><strong><a href="https://observer.com/2015/10/a-pizza-lamborghini-is-giving-away-scholarships-at-these-70-colleges-this-fall/">Push for Pizza</a></strong>: One lucky winner at each school will win a pizza scholarship, AKA free pizza for an entire semester, delivered by a Lamborghizza.</li>
</ol>
<p><img src="https://bucketeer-e05bbc84-baa3-437e-9518-adb32be77984.s3.amazonaws.com/public/images/07b8d1be-de15-48a2-aa21-9c1f66b7fe26_825x550.jpeg" alt="At Brown University (Photo: Push for Pizza)"></p>
<ol start="26">
<li><strong><a href="https://twitter.com/fast/status/1301220870183104514">Fast</a></strong>: $5 hoodies</li>
</ol>
<p><img src="https://bucketeer-e05bbc84-baa3-437e-9518-adb32be77984.s3.amazonaws.com/public/images/8790fbb2-7bcd-4f44-a34b-60ad38932d8a_510x680.jpeg" alt="Image"></p>
<ol start="27">
<li><strong><a href="https://cointelegraph.com/news/13-000-defi-users-have-already-claimed-uniswap-s-new-uni-token">Uniswap</a></strong>: Free token distribution.</li>
</ol>
<h2><strong>7.</strong> Include a r<strong>emarkable viral mechanic</strong></h2>
<ol start="28">
<li><strong><a href="https://www.bizjournals.com/newyork/blog/techflash/2015/01/jet-com-gets-a-big-following-with-equity-giveaway.html">Jet.com</a></strong> gave away 100,000 shares of stock options to the person who referred the most signups [<em>Led to acquiring 350,000 users ahead of launch</em>]</li>
<li><strong><a href="https://viral-loops.com/blog/dropbox-grew-3900-simple-referral-program/">Dropbox</a></strong> gave free storage for you and any friend you refer [<em>Contributed to growth from 100k to 4m users in 15 months</em>]</li>
<li><strong><a href="https://tim.blog/2014/07/21/harrys-prelaunchr-email/">Harry’s</a></strong> gave users had the opportunity to earn free products by sharing an invite with friends. The more friends who signed up using your unique referral link, the bigger the prize you earned. [<em>Led to capturing 100,000 emails in one week</em>]</li>
</ol>
<p><img src="https://bucketeer-e05bbc84-baa3-437e-9518-adb32be77984.s3.amazonaws.com/public/images/b16c8b7a-a518-4410-a8c4-f3cf2c9b4998_1130x645.png" alt="Image from Generating buzz"></p>
<ol start="31">
<li><strong><a href="https://viral-loops.com/blog/robinhood-referral-got-1-million-users/">Robinhood</a></strong> gave users priority access by referring their friends (along with a remarkable value prop, see above). [<em>Led to 1 million users before launch</em>]</li>
</ol>
<p><img src="https://bucketeer-e05bbc84-baa3-437e-9518-adb32be77984.s3.amazonaws.com/public/images/8d44f58b-3e3c-44a8-b6ec-6d61a5418314_521x572.jpeg" alt="robinhood referral"></p>
<ol start="32">
<li><strong><a href="https://chicagoonthecheap.com/ritual-eats-week/#:~:text=In%20order%20to%20be%20eligible,New%20users%20are%20automatically%20eligible.">Ritual</a></strong> gave you $1 lunch (max 4 items) for referring a friend. [“<em>At one point seemingly EVERYONE in the city was in a land grab to refer their friends.</em>”]</li>
</ol>
<h2><strong>8.</strong> Get a remarkable <strong>hard-to-get product to be shared by influencers or press</strong></h2>
<ol start="33">
<li><strong><a href="https://Hey.com">Hey.com</a></strong>: Promoted by <a href="https://twitter.com/dhh">DHH</a> (~400k Twitter followers), while being invite-only [<em>Lead to 180k people waitlist</em>]</li>
<li><strong><a href="https://druriley.com/superhuman-marketing-playbook-9-strategies-19-examples/#0-scarcity-marketing">Superhuman</a>:</strong> Used by an increasing number of tech influencers (which you can see in email signatures), while being invite-only [<em>Led to 275k people waitlist</em>]</li>
<li><strong><a href="https://twitter.com/blakeir/status/1257906709877702658">Clubhouse</a></strong>: Mentioned by an increasing number of tech influencers (which you see on Twitter), while being invite-only</li>
<li><strong><a href="https://people.com/style/thirdlove-extended-bra-launch-waitlist/">ThirdLove</a>:</strong> Launched with press and influencer marketing [<em>Led to 1.3 million people waitlist</em>]</li>
<li><strong><a href="https://thenextweb.com/market-intelligence/2015/03/17/how-oneplus-ones-marketing-made-it-the-most-desirable-phone-in-the-world/">OnePlus One</a>:</strong> A cheap smartphone that you could only get by invitation [<em>Led to selling over 1.5 million units in 12 months</em>]</li>
<li><strong><a href="https://www.fastcompany.com/1671952/the-ux-thinking-behind-mailboxs-800000-person-waiting-list">Mailbox</a>:</strong> Known founder launches a slick new product, while having a visible waitlist [<em>Led to 800k people waitlist</em>]</li>
</ol>
<p><img src="https://bucketeer-e05bbc84-baa3-437e-9518-adb32be77984.s3.amazonaws.com/public/images/f335e676-762c-4149-b547-a5917c070dde_635x300.jpeg" alt="How the biggest consumer apps got their first 1,000 users - Issue 25"></p>
<h2>9. Carry out a remarkable pre-launch tease</h2>
<ol start="39">
<li><strong><a href="https://twitter.com/itiseyemoutheye/status/1276729816469975040">👁️👄👁️</a></strong> : It is what it is [<em>Led to 20,000 email signups and $200,000 for charity</em>]</li>
<li><strong><a href="https://shanebarker.com/blog/product-launch-marketing-ideas/#14_Use_Visual_Countdowns_on_Social_Media">Chobani</a>:</strong> Every day counted down (using different fruit to represent the number of days remaining) to a mysterious announcement — turned out to be new flavors</li>
</ol>
<p><img src="https://bucketeer-e05bbc84-baa3-437e-9518-adb32be77984.s3.amazonaws.com/public/images/072ac7c1-e31c-48fe-a188-04f2ab8e69b5_824x524.jpeg" alt="Chobani Instagram profile product launch marketing ideas"></p>
<ol start="41">
<li><strong><a href="https://abcnews.go.com/GMA/story?id=126341&amp;page=1">Segway</a></strong>: “After nearly a year of speculation, Dean Kamen's mysterious machine — IT — was revealed on ABCNEWS' Good Morning America.”</li>

<h2><strong>10.</strong> Be remarkably <strong>everywhere</strong></h2>
<ol start="42">

</li>

<h2>[58/61] Ideal sprint length, designer vs. PM roles, running PM team meetings, running post-mortems, best product/executive coaches, and much more</h2>
<p><em>👋 Hello and welcome to this week’s edition of ✨<strong>Community Wisdom</strong> ✨ — a weekly email highlighting the most helpful advice from our subscriber Slack community. As always, a b​i​g thank you to <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/kiyanibamba?originalSubdomain=ca">Kiyani</a> for pulling this email together.</em></p>
<h4>💥 Top threads this week</h4>
<ol>
<li>Ideal sprint lengths</li>
<li>Where a PM’s job ends and the product designer’s begins</li>
<li>Running a PM team meeting</li>
<li>Running a leadership team meetings</li>
<li>Running a post-mortem</li>
<li>Great product/executive coaches</li>
</ol>
<h4>🤓 Top reads and listens</h4>
<ol>
<li>Setting up a sales team</li>
<li>Lessons for early-stage founders</li>
<li>Stripe: Platform of Platforms</li>
<li>On product discovery</li>
<li>Takeaways from <em>Are Your Lights On</em>?</li>
</ol>
<p>Let’s dive in!</p>
<h1>💥 Top threads this week</h1>
<h3>1. Ideal sprint lengths</h3>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>Q: Hi all. I'm curious, what cadence do you use for sprints in a startup of 2-3 people? Does 2 weeks make sense or do you go for a shorter cycle?</strong></p>
<p><strong>– Soumya Santhanakrishnan</strong></p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong><a href="https://twitter.com/wplusv">Wayne Vernon</a></strong>: It depends! I’ve worked with 1 and 2-week iterations, though never at a 2-3 person scale, so take this with a grain of salt.</p>
<p>The big question is: What does a sprint mean to you?</p>
<p>Is this just for planning? Agile ceremonies? Are you releasing based on sprints?</p>
<p>My gut says that this ultimately won’t matter at your scale, since with a smaller team the overhead introduced by running shorter iterations is more or less negligible.</p>
<p>But ignoring your specific situation, I hear “one-week sprints” and think “more meetings to stay aligned”, and more meetings are usually bandaids rather than a proper solution.</p>
<p>My short answer is: I’d stick to two weeks because there are better ways to stay on track that doesn’t introduce more overhead.</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://twitter.com/aadilmaan">Aadil Maan</a></strong>: @Wayne Vernon literally read my mind. Love this stance. Moneyball.</p>
<p>I would go further and suggest that at this scale, perhaps you shouldn't worry about sprints at all. Just focus on continuously shipping features out when you feel like you have something. It sounds wrong but Teams don't realize that 2wk sprints aren't just 2wks of coding, there is a lot of planning and administrative work that needs to happen to get those sprints to sing.</p>
<p>At Google, I saw teams ship/sprints daily, 2wks, 6wk, quarter. Android is sometimes multiple quarters to a year.</p>
<p><strong>Soumya Santhanakrishnan</strong>: That's super helpful, thank you! I'm leaning towards not even thinking in terms of sprints. At <a href="https://www.souffle.club">www.souffle.club</a> we’re building a fun Q&amp;A space for the most insightful takes on tech topics. So we end up launching questions every 3 days. Given that timeline, my co-founder and I have been talking about how we should just have a priority for a day and ship ASAP. and if it takes &gt;=3 days then flag and talk about it.</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://productsolving.com">Tyler Wince</a></strong>: FWIW, we ditched Scrum in our 20 person engineering team (3-4 engineers per product team) and just do Kanban now. Everyone is more aligned, attends fewer meetings, and we have more flexibility than we ever did doing Scrum.</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://twitter.com/herzigma">Josh</a></strong>: We're also in the process of ditching formal 2-week sprints and moving to Kanban. Also about 20 engineers so keeping up regular rituals of prioritizing, grooming, standups and retros. Also very interested in trying 6-week sprints, mainly as a tool for more clearly involving engineering in discovery and design.</p>
<p>I rarely find 2-week sprints the right cadence.</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://twitter.com/aadilmaan">Aadil Maan</a></strong>: This thread is magical because engineering teams often equate being agile to 2wk sprints and hearing many of you are ditching it.</p>
<p>So, I actively participated in switching the Google Dart team from quarter shipping to 6wk shipping. There is something goldilocks about 6wks. The first 2wks can give engineers a green field to really dig deep and get dirty with the details, draw plans and then mad dash 4wks to the finish line. I advocate a modified sprint of 8wks. 6wks of engineering with 2wks of testing and converging the work done in the 6wks.</p>
<p>My experience has taught me that Any feature work more than 4wks in estimate needs more grooming.</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://twitter.com/herzigma">Josh</a></strong>: @Aadil Maan I'd love to hear more about 6-week sprints - fell in love with that unicorn after reading <a href="https://basecamp.com/shapeup">Shape Up: Stop Running in Circles and Ship Work that Matters</a> and am still hoping to experience it in the wild.</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://twitter.com/aadilmaan">Aadil Maan</a></strong>: I have lived with 6wks and it’s the magical number. iOS and macOS are developed over 4 back to back sprints 8wks in size. 6wks of development with 2wks of convergence during which no feature work happens, it’s all testing and fixing the work we did in the 6wks. Dart moved away from quarter because it’s easy to drift while 6wks is enough to stay focused and do complex work.</p>
<p>I would love to chat more if you want and I really want to hear about your experience with 6wks. Basecamp, although it gets crap sometimes for their work stances, have some really awesome nuggets. I have read every book they have shipped and each is a fascinating read.</p>
<p><strong>Scott Stenback</strong>: For those of you using Kanban, I was inspired by <a href="https://www.oreilly.com/library/view/lean-from-the/9781941222935/">Lean from the Trenches</a> a few years ago and created the version below for one of my teams.</p>
<p>The On Deck column was populated with cards from the four columns directly to the left.  From there it was the standard left to right flow until Done.</p>
<p>We bent Jira to get it usable for remote but in reality, it was always meant to be on a wall with post-it notes and painter’s tape.</p>
<p><img src="https://bucketeer-e05bbc84-baa3-437e-9518-adb32be77984.s3.amazonaws.com/public/images/8c0153d7-1f06-4c64-9fe1-c63621636619_716x94.png" alt="Image from Ideal sprint length, designer vs. PM roles, running PM team meetings, running"></p>
<p><strong><a href="https://twitter.com/wplusv">Wayne Vernon</a></strong>: Amazing conversation! I’m really happy to see more people moving away from what’s “normal” and to what works best for <em>their</em> situation.</p>
<p>Agile is a collection of tools in your toolbox, not a single hammer. Pick and choose the pieces you need to maximize the likelihood of success for your org, product, and team.</p>
<p><strong>Zachary Cohn</strong>: At 2-3 people, I’d ditch more formal agile and use either an agile ban or a full-on kanban approach. This tends to work well for maximum throughput, which as a 2-3 person team should be your goal. (throughput being defined loosely - engineering, product validation, user research, etc).</p>
<p>Things move too fast at that stage to wait until the end of the sprint to change direction.</p>
<h3>2. Where a PM’s job ends and the product designer’s begins</h3>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>Q:  On your teams, where does a product manager’s job ‘end’ and where does the product designer ‘begin’?</strong></p>
<p><strong>– <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/talsraviv/">Tal Raviv</a></strong></p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong><a href="https://twitter.com/herzigma">Josh</a></strong>: I'm no longer allowed to use Figma.</p>
<p>More seriously, we work side by side, we're both heavily focused on discovering and designing just different focus areas. My observation is that if Product isn't butting heads with Product Design (and product marketing and even engineering) then something's not right. And in this case, the butting heads when you're in a group and see a piece of litter on the ground, and you all bend down to pick it up and bump heads...the leaning in kind.</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://twitter.com/jfedor">Jeff</a></strong>: I like to think of it as co-design. Product brings the context of the problem we're solving and why it matters. Together with Design and Eng, a range of solutions are explored. Eng provides options that are feasible (based on whatever constraints), Design owns the experience of that solution.</p>
<p>For me, Product owns customer discovery and I think it's powerful for Design to own product discovery. But wherever we can, we (Design, Eng, &amp; Product) all ride shotgun on those sessions. Conversations and iterations get us to the solution.</p>
<p>But like @Josh I'm not in Figma to do anything other than comment.</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://twitter.com/wplusv">Wayne Vernon</a></strong>: Varies quite a bit with the experience, skillset, and interests of the product designer. As well as with the product and the type of design work it demands. And with the scale of your org.</p>
<p>On bigger products or products that are more technical (e.g. less hands-on design to be done), I’ve found senior product designers are often happy to spill over into user and market research, which is fine by me.</p>
<p>But like everybody else – I never touch design!</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://twitter.com/archismandas">Archisman Das</a></strong>: Jeff has articulated it really well. I think the job doesn't end, but the roles that PM and Design/Engineering play reverses depending upon the stage you are in. During the Customer Discovery, Design and Engineering play the role of the checker and it is the PM's responsibility to prove that it is the most important problem/opportunity that the team should pursue now. Once that's done, in Product Discovery stage Design and Engineering comes up with the solution and the PM evaluates if it is the best way to deliver value to their customers.</p>
<p>Now, this is an ideal scenario and the reality varies based upon the maturity and strength of the team members.</p>
<p><strong>Zachary Cohn</strong>: I'm actively hiring a product designer right now with the express goal of kicking me out of Adobe XD. I think at one point in an informational interview I said “Please stop me.”</p>
<h3>3. Running a PM team meeting</h3>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>Q: I'd love to learn more about how other teams run PM meetings. We run a weekly PM meeting to increase transparency of efforts and information.</strong></p>
<p><strong>Currently our format is this:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><strong>General announcements - General updates: Company direction, new company-level decisions, etc.</strong></li>
<li><strong>Follow ups from last MTG</strong></li>
<li><strong>Recent / Upcoming launches - Celebrate launches + raise awareness for implications to other parts of product</strong></li>
<li><strong>New efforts / Updates to existing efforts - New efforts or direction for visibility to other PMs. No need to list every single project - just ones beneficial for other PMs.</strong></li>
<li><strong>Helpful reads / info - Slack threads that provide context and info, or good reads for PMs</strong></li>
<li><strong>Other random things</strong></li>
<li><strong>Todos</strong></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>I'd love to hear about other agenda items that have been beneficial in increasing the output of PMs ;- ) Thanks!</strong></p>
<p><strong>– <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/takehiro-tank-mori-a3083487/">Tank</a></strong></p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/alexdou/">Alex Dou</a></strong>: I have a manager friend who has instituted “BB Time” (BB in honor of Brené Brown). The purpose of this time is to be vulnerable and let down the mien/mask of “everything is going smoothly”.</p>
<p>Our PM team has a section carved out for “what feels hard [harder than it should be]?” The purpose of this section is to discuss sticking points up and down the stack: troubleshooting comms with Team X; doing a teardown of a PRD that has raised unexpected questions and gotten “stuck”; trying on versions of a Vision/Strategy.</p>
<p>I think the correct cadence is ~1/week, and even though these might come up in 1:1s (which hopefully you are doing weekly!) I think it’s worth having this crosstalk with other PMs.</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/takehiro-tank-mori-a3083487/">Tank</a></strong>: Nice, thanks @Alex! How long are the meetings btw? I'm also curious to hear what the outcomes of those discussions are -- does it result in senior PMs mentoring more junior PMs or has it been more of a flat discussion?</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://twitter.com/herzigma">Josh</a></strong>: @Alex that's a great model. One (similar) thing I did was start by opening it up for anyone to speak. It gave me a chance to model open listening and we could all practice responding without judgment. It built great trust. @Michael Kellar - keep me honest - what worked well and what should we have done better.</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/amitjklein/">Amit</a></strong>: Are you talking about a cross-functional PM meeting (many PMs?) or a weekly product team meeting led by PM (with eng/design/etc...)?</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/takehiro-tank-mori-a3083487/">Tank</a></strong>: A cross-functional PM meeting with many PMs.</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/amitjklein/">Amit</a></strong>: For business as usual meetings (no major changes upcoming). One way we've done this in the past is a monthly meeting where we:</p>
<ul>
<li>Review of top-level company goals and how we are doing against them</li>
<li>Each PM goes around and shows their key KPI(s), experiments run/lessons learned/upcoming launches (5 min each)</li>
<li>One PM is the guest speaker and presents a challenge they are facing or a key decision they need to make for feedback and we deep dive.</li>
</ul>
<p>We had separate product review meetings (each PM had one weekly) with product leadership that was approval focused (and any PM could attend most usually did to listen in).</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://linkedin.com/in/michael-kellar">Michael Kellar</a></strong>: I think these are awesome opportunities both for cross-functional sharing, as you referenced, but also team building/trust. As @Josh said, one of the best things he accomplished for us, was building a team that really trusted one another, we had that mystical psychological safety and it was huge.</p>
<p>The biggest thing I took away was that making sure you take the time to encourage everyone in the room to participate (or sometimes just wait/let them come to the team), meant we all had a voice, and knew we had each other’s best interests in mind.</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/takehiro-tank-mori-a3083487/">Tank</a></strong>: Nice, that's a great point actually. Our team hasn't really talked too much about establishing psychological safety, but I definitely feel that that is important for a product team.</p>
<p>(I think it's often a challenge since a lot of times product managers are siloed in their own domain).</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://app.slack.com/team/U01GA3RA69F">Jonah</a></strong>: PMing requires strong writing. We've allocated part of our weekly meeting to review a team member's product brief and provide immediate feedback. It has very quickly elevated everyone's clarity in their writing. A secondary benefit has been streamlining our team communications.</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/alexdou/">Alex Dou</a></strong>: @Tank meetings were short. This “what’s hard” line item was budgeted with maybe ~5-10 minutes, with an option (as always) to spin off into a separate thread to avoid ballooning this meeting</p>
<p>I can also speak to some of the outcomes I perceived:</p>
<ul>
<li>Blockers are identified early and loudly - this was during our PM staff meetings, so it was a kind of <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chatham_House_Rule#:~:text=Since%20its%20most%20recent%20refinement,other%20participant%2C%20may%20be%20revealed.">Chatham House Rules</a> space where we could share hardships without worrying about negative reactions. As you might imagine, this helped us decrease wasted cycles, come up with better answers/approaches earlier, etc</li>
<li>Stronger team self-worth/self-esteem - I oftentimes feel pressure to “know everything” (as an aside, one of the things I think is hard about PM is an implicit pressure/assumption to always “be the guy/gal” nb. this is a specific manifestation of the <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spotlight_effect">Spotlight Effect</a>). However, when you only see yourself struggling against something—as we all do in our lives—and everybody else dutifully “knowing everything”, you start to think that you’re the problem. This really helps fight against that</li>
<li>Increased <em>espirit de corps</em> - this is closest to what you were talking about. It was a good opportunity for spontaneous brainstorming and mentorship (not just Srs mentoring Jrs, but also folks with <a href="https://www.ravi-mehta.com/product-manager-roles/">differently spikey radar charts</a> mentoring others).</li>
</ul>
<p>And @Josh, I love that. I think this is one of those tricky things where it has to be modeled by the team lead, otherwise, it’s <em>just</em> on this side of “too weird”. But it really helps Team Leads/Heads of/VPs by fostering that psychological safety that other folks have mentioned already. In the Rands manifesto, this is also an underutilized source of <a href="https://randsinrepose.com/archives/the-signal-network/">Critically Fresh information</a></p>
<p>@Michael Kellar, something you mentioned really piqued my side hobby interest in group psychology and its overlap with meeting facilitation. Do you remember the first time Josh brought this practice to the table? Do you remember your reaction?</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/takehiro-tank-mori-a3083487/">Tank</a></strong>: These are all great resources, thanks @Alex Dou!</p>
<p>Do you remember the first time Josh brought this practice to the table? Do you remember your reaction?</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://linkedin.com/in/michael-kellar">Michael Kellar</a></strong>: I do not recall the first time, but I’d also say it was a practice that changed as the team grew. In the beginning we were very small, just a few people, so I think it was almost an entirely informal meeting, and then as we got bigger, it became a more intentional practice that we appreciated and made sure to maintain.</p>
<h3>4. Running a leadership team meeting</h3>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>Q: We are looking to run better Leadership Team meetings. Right now it feels more like a read-out/progress report rather than a strategic touchpoint. I am looking to get inspiration/advice:</strong></p>
<ul>
<li><strong>What do you believe the purpose of a Leadership meeting is?</strong></li>
<li><strong>How often do you have them? And how long are they?</strong></li>
<li><strong>What is the agenda? How much is communicating plans vs. discussion on bigger problems? Is the meeting used to keep people accountable or do you have other avenues for that?</strong></li>
<li><strong>How do you set the agenda?</strong></li>
<li><strong>Who is represented?</strong></li>
<li><strong>Do you communicate minutes/decisions with the rest of the organization?</strong></li>
</ul>
<p><strong>– <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/benkaye/">Ben Kaye</a></strong></p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/aishwaryab">Aishwarya</a></strong>: We went through a bit of this in late 2019. As a leadership team, you want to typically have 2 “types” of meetings:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Tactical</strong>: Mainly for quick decisions/progress on key events that have been decided, icebergs that may have been spotted. These are typically weekly.</li>
<li><strong>Strategic</strong>: These are again broken into 2 types based on how you run the business. In our case, we have a Monthly Strategic which focuses on pillars that are 1-2 quarter focussed. The other kind is more for Yearly planning activities - what does success look like for next year, what are priorities, headcount management, budget planning etc.</li>
</ul>
<p>Once this was established, some of the questions you ask above - e.g. communicating plans vs discussions/debates fall into their own cadence. E.g. is it a pricing/packaging decision that will affect GTM motions - that’s more suited for a monthly, is it an a/b test on onboarding that. just launched - thats a quick tactical update.</p>
<p><strong>Brian Clare</strong>: We follow the entrepreneurial operating system from the book Traction by Gino Wickman. Works well. Keeps tasks aligned and people accountable. Aligns to quarterly and annual goals (or rocks as they call it)</p>
<p><strong>Soumya Santhanakrishnan</strong>: We did a thing where we took all the progress report stuff out. It's sent on slack every week in a document format and people can ask questions/comment in the doc. The time with leaders in a room is precious, so there'd be one person who's running the meeting who'd decide on 1-2 topics for us to discuss and unblock. Found it to be much more powerful.</p>
<p><strong>Rohit Gupta</strong>: <a href="https://flows.co/">Flows.co</a> helps you run well-managed meetings and stick to agendas and is currently on LTD -<a href="https://pitchground.com/products/flows">Flows - Reinvent Your Meetings details</a></p>
<p><strong><a href="https://zainabghadiyali.substack.com/">Zainab Ghadiyali</a></strong>: The number one goal for a leadership meeting is alignment. Everyone in that group needs to be in lockstep - it is very easy to get misaligned very quickly.</p>
<ul>
<li>Have them as often as needed to make decisions and align on strategy</li>
<li>Always keep an agenda of the top 1-3 things you need to make decisions. Have more than 3? Schedule another meeting</li>
<li>Everyone in the team gets to bring in a topic for decision</li>
<li>Every function</li>
<li>Yes! But filtered version. The company doesn’t have all the context so just sharing notes without context can be confusing for your employees</li>
</ul>
<h3>5. Running a post-mortem</h3>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>Q: Can anyone recommend any articles or just advice in general on how to run a really great post-launch post-mortem for a product?</strong></p>
<p><strong>– <a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/aorshan/">Adam</a></strong></p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/talsraviv/">Tal Raviv</a></strong>: Great question!</p>
<p>The best sessions I’ve seen have given people ample time to think - that means either ask them a few days before to fill things out in a spreadsheet or silent time during the retro to do just that - I’ve seen both, probably depends on the magnitude of what you’re looking back on (a sprint vs. a major launch)</p>
<p>The other principles I’ve seen important have been psychological safety and withholding blame, and creating an atmosphere where talking about a mistake or failure is not seen as political or personal (a good measure is how defensive people get or open they are to talk about something).</p>
<p>This is hard. Would love to see what others have to say!</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://twitter.com/wplusv">Wayne Vernon</a></strong>: As @Tal Raviv suggested, I’d try to offload some of the thinking and brainstorming as homework.</p>
<p>I’ve had luck with doing a pre-meeting survey, distilling the responses down to 3 key themes, and then focusing the discussion on only those themes.</p>
<p>Trying to cover everything gets out of hand fast and you’ll end up talking about so much that it will be impossible to make any meaningful change.</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://linkedin.com/in/walterbudzian">Walter Budzian</a></strong>: I’ll piggyback on what Tal is saying about creating a blameless culture. A great piece to read on this is “The Blame Bias” chapter from a book called Primed to Perform.</p>
<p>To have a high-performing culture where people can do their best work it’s important to examine the root cause of failure in a <em>system</em> not failures in people. It’s not easy - we have a bias to hate the players instead of the game.</p>
<p>The authors illustrate this in a variety of social experiments. A big piece of the puzzle is that a leader's preconceived notions are going to impact outcomes. If, as a leader, you come to the table assuming you have a team that is making mistakes, the team actually performs worse. Conversely, when you have expectations that you have a high performing team with positive intent and you look to the systemic cause of issues, teams perform better.</p>
<p>So, in running those post-mortems, come to the table with high expectations of a team trying to do their best work and collaborate to look at what systems/processes can be improved.</p>
<p>Much more detail in that book, which is my number one recommended read for anyone in management or leadership positions.</p>
<p><strong>Rachel Horowitz</strong>: Agree on all of the above! The other thing that I've found helpful is to circulate a doc with the key dates and events and milestones beforehand so everyone is on the same page about the who and what and can focus on the how and why without getting derailed where different people had different recollections of the facts</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/nishipatel/">Nishi Patel</a></strong>: Agree as well on all of the above. We’ve created retro playbooks in the past to help drive the concept of it being blameless and to help foster a psychologically safe environment. I don’t have the playbook on hand, but here’s one that we based it off of: <a href="https://www.atlassian.com/team-playbook/plays/retrospective">How to Run an Agile Retrospective Meeting with Examples</a>. We also used <a href="https://stickies.io/">stickies.io</a> for remote retros and it worked great.</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/aorshan/">Adam</a></strong>: Thanks so much, everyone! A few of you mentioned circulating materials beforehand. Are there any particular questions or jump-off points you’ve found to be particularly effective when included in the pre-meeting email?</p>
<p><strong><a href="https://www.linkedin.com/in/talsraviv/">Tal Raviv</a></strong>: One tiny tactical thing that helps me is to say the quote “A bad system will beat a good person every time” (attributed to Edward Deming) and emphasize we’re here to fix the system.</p>
<h3>6. Great product/executive coaches</h3>
<blockquote>
<p><strong>Q: Has anyone here worked with a great product/executive coach that they’d recommend to work with a VP of Product at a startup?</strong></p>
<p><strong>– Anonymous</strong></p>
</blockquote>
<p><strong><a href="https://twitter.com/ajwaxman">Adam Waxman</a></strong>: I’ve had a great experience with Donna Lichaw: <a href="https://www.donnalichaw.com/">Donna Lichaw — Leadership and Executive Coaching</a></p>
<p><strong>Jacob Sussman</strong>: I have a call with <a href="https://www.leahfeuer.com/">Leah Feuer</a> next week - she previously worked as a head of product / product advisor before focusing on coaching.</p>
<p><strong>Julia Lipton</strong>: As promised, <a href="https://airtable.com/shrJgawZtGQBv7Xb1/tblKZKSYaUcbbY7UP">here's the list</a>. Feedback welcome! For context, this list is shared with VCs, founders, and friends. Feel free to share it publicly! Also &quot;feature requests&quot; welcome!</p>
<p>You can also <a href="https://airtable.com/shr1HSVTdFDonR75g">nominate a coach</a>. It should take less than 1 min. The fields are optional, the only info I really need are their skills, your vouch, and your email. The rest I can pull.</p>

<p><strong><a href="https://twitter.com/herzigma">Josh</a></strong>: Another great one from David Sacks: <strong><a href="https://sacks.substack.com/p/simple-math-to-set-up-a-sales-team">Simple Math to Set Up a Sales Team</a></strong>.</p>

<p><strong><a href="https://twitter.com/YaakovCohney">Yaakov Cohney</a></strong>: Every Stripe product announcement is incredible on its own, but today's Stripe Capital launch is one that I am sure will lead to many more 'How Can I Invest in Stripe&quot; requests. Anyway, the associated Stratechery is like the perfect wine pairing and one of the best ones I have ever read: <a href="https://stratechery.com/2020/stripe-platform-of-platforms/">Stripe: Platform of Platforms – Stratechery by Ben Thompson</a>.</p>

Until I saw @Lenny Rachitsky’s and @Shreyas Doshi’s threads, I had never appreciated the potential of Twitter as a publishing platform.</p>

It might seem silly at first but the inherent constraints of the medium force you to edit, optimize, and proofread every word (or character!). It’s a great way to not only challenge your writing skills, but to organize your thoughts.</p>
<p>Tiago Forte’s <a href="https://fortelabs.co/blog/progressive-summarization-a-practical-technique-for-designing-discoverable-notes/">progressive summarization</a> technique is a great tool for capturing, filtering, and writing about what you read.</p>
